Note LSAT Lab\'s feature vocab is RICHER/different from LSAT U\'s 60 (108 distinct tags incl. topics like Science/Medical/Animals, reasoning like "Conditional Logic", and flaw subtypes) — will need mapping before importing into LSAT U.\r\n\r\n# Activities Design System\r\n\r\n> The visual + structural contract for the Activities Centre (standalone skill\r\n> drills like Negation Station). Activities **fit the bootcamp\'s warm tactile\r\n> workbook** (`DESIGN.md`): same warm paper surfaces, same Inter + Georgia type,\r\n> same soft depth and calm motion. The one thing each activity keeps for itself\r\n> is a single accent hue. Strategy, users, voice, and anti-references come from\r\n> `PRODUCT.md`. Plain-language term definitions live in `ACTIVITIES-CONTEXT.md`.\r\n>\r\n> Status: v0.2. Covers the activity homepage (intro). Running and completion\r\n> anatomy are intentionally not specified yet (see TODO at the end).\r\n>\r\n> Changed in v0.2 (2026-06-14): retired the standalone cool-emerald "distinct\r\n> world." Activities now share the bootcamp\'s warm workbook palette and type;\r\n> only the accent stays per-activity. Simpler homepage: the loud two-tone\r\n> full-bleed banner is gone, replaced by a calm warm-paper reading column.\r\n\r\n## 1. Principles\r\n\r\n1. **Same family as the workbook.** Activities are not a separate visual world. They use the bootcamp\'s warm cream paper canvas, warm ink, layered paper surfaces, and soft depth, so a drill feels native to the rest of the product.\r\n2. **One accent per activity.** Structure, type, spacing, radius, and motion are fixed and identical across activities and shared with the workbook. Each activity supplies a single ac…3947 tokens truncated…ility** of the Author\'s Explanation?\r\n\r\n- If no, does it use "covariation" data points, or some other means?  \r\n  (covariation means stuff like _No Cause, No Effect_ as a strengthener, or stuff like _Cause w/o Effect / Effect w/o Cause_ as a weakener)\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 5. Assumption Family (Medium) Set 3\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Assumption Family (Medium) Set 3\r\ncategory: Process\r\nestimated_time: 56 mins\r\nquestion_count: 6\r\nquestion_type:\r\n  - Sufficient Assumption\r\n  - Necessary Assumption\r\n  - Principle-Strengthen\r\n  - Strengthen\r\n  - Weaken\r\n  - Flaw\r\n  - Evaluate\r\nreasoning_type:\r\n  - Conditional Logic\r\n  - Comparisons\r\n  - Causation\r\nquestion_feature: Three Main Reasoning Types\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n# The three main Reasoning Types\r\n\r\n**Conditional Logic** - Correct answers address the internal logic:\r\n\r\n- assumed links between ideas\r\n- if/then rules\r\n- a missing fact needed to trigger a rule\r\n- valid and invalid conditional inferences\r\n\r\n**Comparisons** - Correct answers address assumed similarities or differences between two things:\r\n\r\n- part vs. whole\r\n- sample vs. general population\r\n- then vs. now\r\n- this situation vs. that situation\r\n- this group vs. that group\r\n\r\nOr they address the net gain / loss of a comparison:\r\n\r\n- doing this thing vs. not doing this thing\r\n- this thing vs. that thing\r\n\r\n**Causation** - Correct answers relate to one of these two angles:\r\n\r\n- alternate explanations for the curious background fact\r\n- the plausibility of the author\'s explanation for it.\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 6 questions, untimed.\r\n\r\nIf it feels like you\'re dealing with Rules, or **if / then logic**, then focus on understanding the If / Then rule. What idea goes on the **Left** of the conditional arrow? What idea goes on the **Right?** When we\'re using **Conditional Logic** to "prove an argument", the **Left** side should be triggered by facts we know from the Evidence (or Answer Choice), and the **Right** side of the rule should match the Conclusion\'s language.\r\n\r\nIf it feels like you\'re dealing with a **Comparison**, think about weakening with potential differences or strengthening by affirming some similarity. If you\'re dealing with a Net Gain / Loss type of Comparison, think about weakening with potential downsides or strengthening with "equal or better" ideas.\r\n\r\nIf the author is interpreting the **Causal** significance of some Curious Fact (a weird asymmetry exists, or a study that has some interesting data), take time to understand the Author\'s Explanation, think about what kind evidence would help make that Explanation seem more/less plausible, and be sure to consider Alternate Explanations for that same Curious Fact.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 6. Causal Difference-Maker (set 2)\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Causal Difference-Maker (set 2)\r\ncategory: Process\r\nestimated_time: 50 mins\r\nquestion_count: 10\r\nquestion_type:\r\n  - Most Supported\r\n  - Must Be True\r\n  - Necessary Assumption\r\nreasoning_type: Causation\r\nquestion_feature: Causal Difference-Maker\r\nstart_button_label: Continue\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n**Causal Difference-Maker** is a very malleable term we can use in a range of difference contexts. It\'s a useful mental construct because when the stimulus presents something we could describe this way, the correct answer is almost always testing our understanding of the causal difference-maker. Here are some of the ways it shows up.\r\n\r\n- when you\'re hearing about the linchpin, the crucial element, the clutch factor that\'s involved in making some decision or producing some effect, you\'re talking about a reaaaal **causal difference-maker**\r\n\r\n_you\'re trying to decide whether to stay at the library past midnight to study more by yourself or to call it a night and walk back with your study buddies to the parking lot. your most important criterion is feeling safe, so that\'s the causal difference-maker that makes you say, "wait up, guys. I\'m leaving, too."_\r\n\r\n- when you\'re given a causal relationship and you have a way to apply it to some comparison\r\n\r\n_Having a taller frame makes your center of gravity higher. Ann is taller than Martin_ -- you\'ll end up thinking to yourself, "this cause they told me about will **make a difference** between the two things in this comparison. Ann\'s center of gravity will be higher."\r\n\r\n- when you\'re trying to solve a causal mystery, you\'re searching for the explanation or the **causal difference-maker**\r\n\r\n_Ever since Randy came back from summer camp, he\'s had an itchy rash._ (Maybe it\'s poison ivy? Some reaction to the water from the lake? Bed bugs from the bunks? Whatever it is, the camp probably caused his rash)\r\n\r\n- when the test identifies factors that made a key difference, they often test our understanding of that with answers that feel like, "If that **causal difference-maker hadn\'t occurred,** then things would be different"\r\n\r\n_I saw Bernadette doing a call exercise I had never seen, so I started copying it. Because of this, I was able to dunk a basketball by the end of the summer. (had I not seen her call exercise, I probably wouldn\'t have dunked by the end of summer)_\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 10 questions untimed\r\n\r\nLook for causal verbs, like "promotes, inhibits, increases, lowers, contributes to, influences". Look for ", -ing" constructions that convey causation, such as "Sarah yelled at the top of her lungs, shaking the vase on the shelf".\r\n\r\nLook for causal connector language that tells you that the previous sentence has a causal relationship to the present sentence, like "because of this, due to this, this allows, this makes possible, accordingly, that is why".\r\n\r\nLook for one Difference that could potentially explain another Difference.\r\n\r\nApply cause/effect relationships to two halves of a comparison (one that has more of the causal factor, one that has less).\r\n\r\nLook for someone to say _what really matters_, what the key concern is when making a judgment, a decision, a classification.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 7. Describing Arguments (set 2)\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Describing Arguments (set 2)\r\ncategory: Pacing\r\nestimated_time: 1 hr, 10 mins\r\nquestion_count: 10\r\ntimed_length: 15 mins\r\ntime_accommodations:\r\n  1.5x: 23 mins\r\n  2x: 30 mins\r\nsource_tests:\r\n  - PT 65\r\n  - PT 71\r\n  - June 2007\r\ndifficulty_level: 1-5\r\nquestion_type:\r\n  - Role\r\n  - Method\r\n  - Main Conclusion\r\nquestion_feature: Describing Arguments\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nThis 10 question set is comprised of three questions from the Match Family, which test our ability to understand what role certain claims are playing: Role, Method, and Main Conclusion.\r\n\r\n# Role and Main Conclusion\r\n\r\nMost questions "hide" the conclusion in one of two ways:\r\n\r\n- it\'s the **very first claim**, or\r\n- it\'s attached to some **pivot / rebuttal**\r\n\r\n**Opinion** indicators and **Pivot** indicators are very helpful for finding the parts of the argument.\r\n\r\nWhen we\'re assessing whether something is a conclusion (be it main conclusion or intermediate conclusion), we should ask ourselves,\r\n\r\n"**Is this Supported?** Why should we believe this?"  \r\nIf no support it provided, then it isn\'t a conclusion.\r\n\r\n# Method\r\n\r\nThese use a lot of abstract verbiage to describe parts of arguments. We want to not only learn how to read this type of language but also to start to think more in terms of this language. When you\'re using abstract language, you\'re speaking about general forms / archetypes, and the magic of getting better at standardized tests is getting better at recognizing the general forms / archetypes that show up a lot on that test.\r\n\r\nWe should get used to the most typical types of reasoning moves:\r\n\r\n- Analogies / Distinctions\r\n- Suggesting / Ruling out Alternatives\r\n- Overlooked Consideration / Dubious Assumption\r\n- Flawed Implications of a Claim or Plan\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nLength -- **10 questions, 15 mins** (1.5x = 23, 2x = 30)  \r\nTypes -- **Role, Method, and Main Conclusion**  \r\nTests -- **65, 71, and June 2007**  \r\nLevels -- **1 - 4**\r\n\r\n- Try to answer all 10 questions in 15 minutes.\r\n- Flag a problem every time you feel less confident in your answer or feel like you struggled to get there.\r\n- When your timer goes off, flip back through the questions via the Results page and take a second look, hit SHOW ATTEMPT, and check out the Explanation (if there is one).\r\n- Add any takeaways you want to the NOTES window (a movable, resizable popup), by clicking on the icon in the lower left of the page.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 8. Agree/Disagree - Set 2\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Agree/Disagree - Set 2\r\ncategory: Pacing\r\nestimated_time: 40 mins\r\nquestion_count: 5\r\ntimed_length: 8 mins\r\ntime_accommodations:\r\n  1.5x: 12 mins\r\n  2x: 16 mins\r\nsource_tests: 50-54\r\ndifficulty_level: 2-4\r\nquestion_type: Point at Issue / Agree-Disagree\r\nquestion_feature: Timed Agree/Disagree\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n## Find the one claim they actually clash on\r\n\r\nAgree / Disagree questions (also called Point at Issue) give you two speakers. The correct answer is the single statement where one speaker would **affirm** it and the other would **deny** it.\r\n\r\nMost wrong answers fail one of two tests:\r\n- Only one speaker addresses it, so we cannot know the other\'s view.\r\n- Both speakers would actually take the same side.\r\n\r\nYou are not hunting for conclusion versus support here. You are mapping what each person commits to, then finding the one point of genuine collision.\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n## Try these 5 in 8 minutes\r\n\r\n(1.5x = 12, 2x = 16). Read each person\'s paragraph for what they claim, not for argument structure.\r\n\r\nThen, for each claim the first speaker made, ask:\r\n- Did the second speaker **agree**, **disagree**, or give **no comment**?\r\n- If no one stated it outright, would the first speaker\'s reasoning force a view the second speaker rejects?\r\n\r\nThe right answer is the claim where one says yes and the other says no. Run each choice through a simple check: could both speakers fill in a clear "I agree" or "I disagree"? If either has to shrug, that answer is out.\r\n\r\n## 9. Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 1\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 1\r\ncategory: Pacing\r\nestimated_time: 1 hr, 10 mins\r\nquestion_count: 10\r\ntimed_length: 15 mins\r\ntime_accommodations:\r\n  1.5x: 22.5 mins\r\n  2x: 30 mins\r\ndifficulty_level: 2-4\r\nquestion_type: Mixed LR question types\r\nreasoning_type: Conditional Logic\r\nquestion_feature: Conditional Logic\r\nstart_button_label: Continue\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n- Find **Conditional Indicator** words and use them as a way to organize how you write the If / Then rule.\r\n\r\n- Write or think through the contrapositive as well.\r\n\r\n- Beware Illegal Reversals and Negations.\r\n\r\nThe LR question types that most often would prompt us to consider diagramming the Conditional Logic are\r\n\r\n- Must Be True\r\n- Most Supported\r\n- Assumption (mostly Sufficient, but some Necessary)\r\n- Parallel / Parallel Flaw\r\n- Principle-Strengthen\r\n- Principle-Conform\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n**Length:** 10 questions  \r\n**Theme:** Conditional Logic  \r\n**Levels:** 2 - 4  \r\n**Time:** 15 mins (1.5x = 22.5, 2x = 30)\r\n\r\nTry to get through them timed and then review them untimed before you check your original answer and look at the explanation.\r\n\r\n_Conditional Logic, by question type:_\r\n\r\n- **Principle** (the left side of the Principle matches the Evidence, the right side matches the Conclusion)\r\n\r\n- **Parallel** (diagramming these arguments with "algebraic recipes" allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers)\r\n\r\n- **Assumption** (the conclusion gives us an A --> Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A --> Z path ... or, the evidence will provide a conditional rule that potentially could output the conclusion, but we still need an answer choice to tell us something in order to clearly trigger that rule.)\r\n\r\n- **Must Be True / Most Supported** (multiple conditionals often chain together, and/or we can apply a conditional rule to some provided fact, usually because the fact triggers the contrapositive of the rule)\r\n\r\n- **Flaw / Parallel Flaw** (Flaw + Conditional Logic usually means the _Necessary vs. Sufficient_ flaw, in which an author presents a conditional relationship in the Evidence but then interprets it using an illegal Negation or Reversal. Much less frequently, the flaw involves messing up Or / And stuff in the rule.)\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 10. Evaluate - First Taste\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Evaluate - First Taste\r\ncategory: Process\r\nestimated_time: 56 mins\r\nquestion_count: 7\r\nquestion_type: Evaluate\r\nquestion_feature: First Taste\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\nA correct answer on **Evaluate** asks a relevant question. How do we know if it\'s relevant? We know because if we answered the question one way, we would Strengthen the argument. If we answered it the opposite way, we would Weaken the argument.\r\n\r\nThe more important one, though, is the potential to weaken. So, if you want you can just listen for the weakening potential. Think to yourself, "Which of these questions could I answer in a way that would most Weaken the argument?"\r\n\r\n# Wrong Answers\r\n\r\n**No Impact** - they pose a question that we could answer any way we\'d like and it wouldn\'t affect the argument.\r\n\r\n**Too Specific / Strong** - they\'re requesting an exact figure or measurement or posing an extreme question that wouldn\'t Weaken if the answer were \'no\'.\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 7 questions, untimed.\r\n\r\nEach answer choice poses a question that could be answered in at least 2 different ways. We want one of those answers to _weaken_ the argument. So don\'t pick an answer choice unless you hear it asking a question that could be answered in a way that would weaken the argument.\r\n\r\nIn general, expect Evaluate arguments to deal with dubious _comparisons_ and authors who are trying to offer _causal_ explanations for some curious fact.\r\n\r\nTry to use your Weaken mindset to evaluate the argument. Think:\r\n\r\n_Given_ this evidence  \r\n_How can I argue_ the anti-conclusion?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 11. Evaluate (set 1)\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Evaluate (set 1)\r\ncategory: Process\r\nestimated_time: 40 mins\r\nquestion_count: 4\r\nquestion_type: Evaluate\r\nquestion_feature: Evaluate\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\nA correct answer on **Evaluate** asks a relevant question.\r\n\r\nHow do we know if it\'s relevant? We know because if we answered the question one way, we would Strengthen the argument. If we answered it the opposite way, we would Weaken the argument.\r\n\r\nThe more important one, though, is the potential to weaken. So, if you want you can just listen for the weakening potential. Think to yourself, "Which of these questions could I answer in a way that would most Weaken the argument?"\r\n\r\n# Wrong Answers\r\n\r\n**No Impact** - they pose a question that we could answer any way we\'d like and it wouldn\'t affect the argument.\r\n\r\n**Too Specific / Strong** - they\'re requesting an exact figure or measurement or posing an extreme question that wouldn\'t Weaken if the answer were \'no\'.\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 4 questions, untimed.\r\n\r\nEach answer choice poses a question that could be answered in at least 2 different ways. We want one of those answers to _weaken_ the argument. So don\'t pick an answer choice unless you hear it asking a question that could be answered in a way that would weaken the argument.\r\n\r\nIn general, expect Evaluate arguments to deal with dubious _comparisons_ and authors who are trying to offer _causal_ explanations for some curious fact.\r\n\r\nTry to use your Weaken mindset to evaluate the argument. Think:\r\n\r\n_Given_ this evidence  \r\n_How can I argue_ the anti-conclusion?\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 12. Most Supported + Causal Difference-Maker\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Most Supported + Causal Difference-Maker\r\ncategory: Patterns\r\nestimated_time: 46 mins\r\nquestion_count: 4\r\nquestion_type: Most Supported\r\nreasoning_type: Causation\r\nquestion_feature: Causal Difference-Maker\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\n**Causal Difference-Maker** is a fuzzy term  \r\nwe use primarily for Most Supported\r\n\r\nThe most common type of reasoning tested on Most Supported questions is Causality, so whenever we read the stimulus on a Most Supported question, we listen closely for any explicit or implicit cause/effect relationships.\r\n\r\nThese tend to be expressed in three ways:\r\n\r\n- **Causal verbs** (_leads to, results in, contributes to, increases, limits, etc._) When they use causal verbs, they often use more than one, setting up a causal chain.\r\n\r\n- **Causal connectors** between adjacent claims (_Because of this, this allows, this makes possible, accordingly, thereby, etc._) This is a sneaky way of conveying causality. We might hear something like, "Ants shed their hind legs and grow new ones every two years. This cycle allows the ant to avoid suffering from arthritis." This sort of causal connection reveals that an ant\'s hind legs influence whether or not they suffer from arthritis.\r\n\r\n- Presenting two differences, with the suggestion that **one difference explains the other**. (for example: _Tim and Jill were both struggling with reading comp. Jill, unlike Tim, decided to try meditating shortly after practicing each RC passage. Jill no longer struggles with RC, though Tim still does._) This seems to be suggesting that meditation was a Causal Difference-Maker when it comes to Jill getting better at RC. We certainly can\'t _prove_ that, but if you\'ve been told about Difference X (Jill does meditation, Tim doesn\'t) then it\'s supportable to say it might be the reason for Difference Y (Jill no longer struggles with RC).\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 4 questions untimed\r\n\r\nLook for causal verbs, like "promotes, inhibits, increases, lowers, contributes to, influences". Look for ", -ing" constructions that convey causation, such as "Sarah yelled at the top of her lungs, shaking the vase on the shelf".\r\n\r\nLook for causal connector language that tells you that the previous sentence has a causal relationship to the present sentence, like "because of this, due to this, this allows, this makes possible, accordingly, that is why".\r\n\r\nLook for one Difference that could potentially explain another Difference.\r\n\r\n---\r\n\r\n## 13. Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 2\r\n\r\n### Metadata\r\n\r\n```yaml\r\ntitle: Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 2\r\ncategory: Process\r\nestimated_time: 56 mins\r\nquestion_count: 7\r\nquestion_type:\r\n  - Sufficient Assumption\r\n  - Principle-Strengthen\r\nreasoning_type: Conditional Logic\r\nquestion_feature: Conditional Logic\r\nstart_button_label: Start\r\n```\r\n\r\n### Pre-Start Instruction Card\r\n\r\n#### Left Column\r\n\r\nThe problems in this set all contain **Conditional Indicator** words that we can use to correctly write an If / Then rule, and its contrapositive.\r\n\r\nTrap answers will tempt us to make illegal Reversals or Negations.\r\n\r\nThe LR question types most commonly involve conditionals:\r\n\r\n- Must Be True\r\n- Most Supported\r\n- Assumption (mostly Suff, but some Nec)\r\n- Parallel / Parallel Flaw\r\n- Principle-Strengthen / Principle-Conform\r\n\r\n#### Right Column\r\n\r\n# Try these 7 problems, untimed,\r\n\r\n(most are Sufficient Assumption or Principle-Strengthen)\r\n\r\nFor many of these problems, you might feel like you don\'t really need to write anything down to think your way through them. That\'s great! On the actual test, we will mostly use diagramming conditional logic as a last resort. But the way to get better at doing these _mentally_ is to do them _physically_ for a while.\r\n\r\n'}]

in 9 minutes\\n\\n(1.5x = 14 mins, 2x = 18 mins)  \\nParallel tends to take longer than Paradox, so pace accordingly\\n\\n## PARADOX\\n\\n- We\'re not reading an argument.\\n- The question stem will tell us what surprising idea we need to explain, or the final sentence of the stimulus will use a pivot word like \\"but, yet, however\\" to show us the surprising idea\\n- Figure out what background fact, if any, makes this idea a surprise. Frame your thinking like this:\\n\\n_Given that .. background fact(s),_  \\n_How can we explain ... surprising claim?_\\n\\n## PARALLEL\\n\\n- We are reading an argument.\\n- Our job is to see the structure or reasoning pattern behind the argument.\\n- If it\'s possible to describe the reasoning pattern conversationally (e.g. \\"The author is arguing that if the extra effort has diminishing returns, it ain\'t worth it\\") go for it.\\n- But if we see _Conditionals, Quantifiers, or Repeating Ideas_, then it\'s usually better to create a little algebraic recipe for the argument.\\n- Once you have a recipe for the argument, articulate the _first_ ingredient you plan to look for. Are you looking to see if there are two \\"most\\" claims? Are you checking to see whether the Conclusion has the same _probably_ strength as the original? Are you seeing whether there are two conditional premises that chain together?\\n- Be suspicious of answers that have similar topics or other cheap superficial similarities."\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:112:      "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:115:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:128:        "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:154:      "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:157:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:171:        "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:328:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:373:      "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:383:      "left_md": "# The three main Reasoning Structures\\n\\n**Conditional Logic** - Correct answers address the author\'s internal logic\\n\\n- assumed links between ideas\\n- if/then rules\\n- a missing fact needed to trigger…1999 tokens truncated…amming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1191:      "left_md": "# The three main Reasoning Types\\n\\n**Conditional Logic** - Correct answers address the internal logic:\\n\\n- assumed links between ideas\\n- if/then rules\\n- a missing fact needed to trigger a rule\\n- valid and invalid conditional inferences\\n\\n**Comparisons** - Correct answers address assumed similarities or differences between two things:\\n\\n- part vs. whole\\n- sample vs. general population\\n- then vs. now\\n- this situation vs. that situation\\n- this group vs. that group\\n\\nOr they address the net gain / loss of a comparison:\\n\\n- doing this thing vs. not doing this thing\\n- this thing vs. that thing\\n\\n**Causation** - Correct answers relate to one of these two angles:\\n\\n- alternate explanations for the curious background fact\\n- the plausibility of the author\'s explanation for it.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1192:      "right_md": "# Try these 6 questions, untimed.\\n\\nIf it feels like you\'re dealing with Rules, or **if / then logic**, then focus on understanding the If / Then rule. What idea goes on the **Left** of the conditional arrow? What idea goes on the **Right?** When we\'re using **Conditional Logic** to \\"prove an argument\\", the **Left** side should be triggered by facts we know from the Evidence (or Answer Choice), and the **Right** side of the rule should match the Conclusion\'s language.\\n\\nIf it feels like you\'re dealing with a **Comparison**, think about weakening with potential differences or strengthening by affirming some similarity. If you\'re dealing with a Net Gain / Loss type of Comparison, think about weakening with potential downsides or strengthening with \\"equal or better\\" ideas.\\n\\nIf the author is interpreting the **Causal** significance of some Curious Fact (a weird asymmetry exists, or a study that has some interesting data), take time to understand the Author\'s Explanation, think about what kind evidence would help make that Explanation seem more/less plausible, and be sure to consider Alternate Explanations for that same Curious Fact.\\n\\n---"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1205:        "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1344:    "title": "Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 1",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1371:      "Conditional Diagramming"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1374:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1378:      "left_md": "- Find **Conditional Indicator** words and use them as a way to organize how you write the If / Then rule.\\n\\n- Write or think through the contrapositive as well.\\n\\n- Beware Illegal Reversals and Negations.\\n\\nThe LR question types that most often would prompt us to consider diagramming the Conditional Logic are\\n\\n- Must Be True\\n- Most Supported\\n- Assumption (mostly Sufficient, but some Necessary)\\n- Parallel / Parallel Flaw\\n- Principle-Strengthen\\n- Principle-Conform",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1379:      "right_md": "**Length:** 10 questions  \\n**Theme:** Conditional Logic  \\n**Levels:** 2 - 4  \\n**Time:** 15 mins (1.5x = 22.5, 2x = 30)\\n\\nTry to get through them timed and then review them untimed before you check your original answer and look at the explanation.\\n\\n_Conditional Logic, by question type:_\\n\\n- **Principle** (the left side of the Principle matches the Evidence, the right side matches the Conclusion)\\n\\n- **Parallel** (diagramming these arguments with \\"algebraic recipes\\" allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers)\\n\\n- **Assumption** (the conclusion gives us an A --> Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A --> Z path ... or, the evidence will provide a conditional rule that potentially could output the conclusion, but we still need an answer choice to tell us something in order to clearly trigger that rule.)\\n\\n- **Must Be True / Most Supported** (multiple conditionals often chain together, and/or we can apply a conditional rule to some provided fact, usually because the fact triggers the contrapositive of the rule)\\n\\n- **Flaw / Parallel Flaw** (Flaw + Conditional Logic usually means the _Necessary vs. Sufficient_ flaw, in which an author presents a conditional relationship in the Evidence but then interprets it using an illegal Negation or Reversal. Much less frequently, the flaw involves messing up Or / And stuff in the rule.)\\n\\n---"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1402:        "Conditional Diagramming"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1527:    "title": "Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 2",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:1539:      "Conditional all contain **Conditional Indicator** words that we can use to correctly write an If / Then rule, and its contrapositive.\\n\\nTrap answers will tempt us to make illegal Reversals or or the evidence gives us a conditional rule that is being used in drawing the conclusion, but we still need an answer choice to tell us something in order to clearly trigger that rule.)\\n\\n- **Must Be True / Most Supported** (multiple conditionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the Beware the temptation to make illegal Backwards or Opposite moves.\\n\\nHow does Conditional Logic show up, by question type?\\n\\n- **Principle** (the left side of the Principle matches the Evidence, the right side matches the Conclusion)\\n\\n- **Parallel** (diagramming these arguments with \\"algebraic recipes\\" allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers)\\n\\n- **Assumption** (the conclusion gives us an A --> Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A --> Z path / or the evidence gives us a conditional rule that potentially could output the conclusion, but we still need an answer choice to tell us something in order to clearly trigger that rule.)\\n\\n- **Must Be True / Most Supported** (multiple conditionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the contrapositive-outcome)\\n\\n- **Flaw / Parallel Flaw** (Flaw + Conditional Logic usually means the **Necessary vs. Sufficient** flaw, which is using a rule in an illegal Backwards or Opposite fashion. Sometimes it involves messing up Or / And stuff)",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2568:        "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2710:      "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2713:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2726:        "Conditional Conclusion"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2792:      "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2799:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2803:      "left_md": "# The three main Reasoning Structures\\n\\n**Conditional Logic** - Correct answers address the author\'s internal logic.\\n\\n- assumed links between ideas\\n- if/then rules\\n- a missing fact needed to trigger a rule\\n- valid and invalid conditional inferences\\n\\n**Comparisons** - Correct answers address assumed similarities or differences.\\n\\n- part vs. whole\\n- sample vs. general population\\n- then vs. now\\n- this situation vs. that situation\\n- this group vs. that group\\n\\nOr they address the net gain / loss of a comparison:\\n\\n- doing this thing vs. not doing this thing\\n- this thing vs. that thing\\n- weighing the positives vs. the negatives\\n\\n**Causation** - Correct answers address one of these two pressure points.\\n\\n- alternate explanations for some Curious Comparison\\n- the plausibility of the author\'s explanation for it.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2804:      "right_md": "# Try these 7 questions, untimed.\\n\\nWhen we\'re using **Conditional Logic** to \\"prove an argument\\", the **Left** side should be triggered by facts we know from the Evidence (or Answer Choice), and the **Right** side of the rule should match the Conclusion\'s language. If we want to weaken an argument\'s internal logic, then we\'re showing how the Left side is compatible with the **opposite** of the Right side (the Premise is compatible with the Anti-Conclusion).\\n\\nIf it feels like you\'re dealing with a **Comparison**, think about weakening with potential differences or strengthening by affirming some similarity. If you\'re dealing with a Net Gain / Loss type of Comparison, think about weakening with potential downsides or strengthening with \\"equal or better\\" ideas.\\n\\nIf the author is interpreting the **Causal** significance of some Curious Comparison (a weird asymmetry exists, or a study that has some interesting data), take time to understand the Author\'s Explanation, but then consider Alternate Explanations for the same curious fact (is the author confusing cause and effect? is the author failing to recognize that two things are linked to some third factor?)\\n\\n---"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:2817:        "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:3003:    "title": "Conditional Logic (Medium) Set 5",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:3030:      "Conditional Diagramming",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:3034:      "Conditional Logic"\r\nprojects/lsatu/src/data\\guidedDrillsLocalSeed.ts:3038:      "left_md": "- Focus on finding **Conditional Indicator** words and using them as a way to organize how you write the If / Then rule.\\n- Write or think through the contrapositive as well.\\n- Beware the temptation to make illegal Backwards or Opposite moves.\\n\\nHow does Conditional Logic show up, by question type?\\n\\n- **Principle** (the left side of the Principle matches the Evidence, the right side matches the Conclusion)\\n\\n- **Parallel** (diagramming these arguments with \\"algebraic recipes\\" allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers)\\n\\n- **Assumption** (the conclusion gives us an A --> Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A --> Z path / or the evidence gives us a conditional rule that potentially could output the conclusion, but we still need an answer choice to tell us something in order to clearly trigger that rule.)\\n\\n- **Must Be True / Most Supported** (multiple conditionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the contrapositive-outcome)\\n\\n- **Flaw / Parallel Flaw** (Flaw + Conditional Logic usually means the **Necessary vs. Sufficient** flaw, which is using a rule in an illegal Backwards or Opposite fashion. they sit under their own\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\components\\classroom\\classroom.css:1939:.drill-observatory-root .drill-observatory-records {\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\components\\classroom\\classroom.css:2649:/* The shell\'s own label voice, not the hub\'s mono micro-label: this bar is\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\components\\drill\\DrillLaunchGate.tsx:37:import { recordExposures } from \'@/lib/questionExposure\';\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\components\\drill\\DrillLaunchGate.tsx:133:          void recordExposures(user.id, qids).catch((error) => {\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\components\\drill\\DrillLaunchGate.tsx:134:            console.error(\'[DrillLaunchGate] recordExposures failed\', error);\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:5: * string is a verbatim transcription: do not edit, paraphrase, or reflow any\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:106:      "Until about 1970, anyone who wanted to write a comprehensive history of medieval English law as it actually affected women would have found a dearth of published books or articles concerned with specific legal topics relating to women and derived from extensive research in actual court records. This is a serious deficiency, since court records are of vital importance in discovering how the law actually affected women, as opposed to how the law was intended to affect them or thought to affect them. These latter questions can be answered by consulting such sources as treatises, commentaries, and statutes; such texts were what most scholars of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries concentrated on whenever they did write about medieval law. But these sources are of little help in determining, for example, how often women’s special statutory privileges were thwarted by intimidation or harassment, or how often women managed to evade special statutory limitations. And, quite apart from provisions designed to apply only, or especially, to women, they cannot tell us how general law affected the female half of the population—how women defendants and plaintiffs were treated in the courts in practice when they tried to exercise the rights they shared with men. Only quantitative studies of large numbers of cases would allow even a guess at the answers to these questions, and this scholarly work has been attempted by few.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:107:      "One can easily imagine why. Most medieval English court records are written in Latin or Anglo-Norman French and have never been published. The sheer volume of material to be sifted is daunting: there are over 27,500 parchment pages in the common plea rolls of the thirteenth century alone, every page nearly three feet long, and written often front and back in highly stylized court hand. But the difficulty of the sources, while it might appear to explain why the relevant scholarship has not been undertaken, seems actually to have deterred few: the fact is that few historians have wanted to write anything approaching women’s legal history in the first place. Most modern legal historians who have written on one aspect or another of special laws pertaining to women have begun with an interest in a legal idea or event or institution, not with a concern for how it affected women. Very few legal historians have started with an interest in women’s history that they might have elected to pursue through various areas of general law. And the result of all this is that the current state of our scholarly knowledge relating to law and the medieval Englishwoman is still fragmentary at best, though the situation is slowly improving."\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:152:      "In studying the autobiographies of Native Americans, most scholars have focused on as-told-to life histories that were solicited, translated, recorded, and edited by non-Native American collaborators—that emerged from “bicultural composite authorship.” Limiting their studies to such written documents, these scholars have overlooked traditional, preliterate modes of communicating personal history. In addition, they have failed to address the cultural constructs of the highly diverse Native American peoples, who prior to contact with nonindigenous cultures did not share with Europeans the same assumptions about self, life, and writing that underlie the concept of an autobiography—that indeed constitute the English word’s root meaning.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:153:      "The idea of self was, in a number of pre-contact Native American cultures, markedly inclusive: identity was not merely individual, but also relational to a society, a specific landscape, and the cosmos. Within these cultures, the expression of life experiences tended to be oriented toward current events: with the participation of fellow tribal members, an individual person would articulate, reenact, or record important experiences as the person lived them, a mode of autobiography seemingly more fragmented than the European custom of writing down the recollections of a lifetime. Moreover, expression itself was not a matter of writing but of language, which can include speech and signs. Oral autobiography comprised songs, chants, stories, and even the process whereby one repeatedly took on new names to reflect important events and deeds in one’s life. Dance and drama could convey personal history; for example, the advent of a vision to one person might require the enactment of that vision in the form of a tribal pageant.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:154:      "One can view as autobiographical the elaborate tattoos that symbolized a warrior’s valorous deeds, and such artifacts as a decorated shield that communicated the accomplishments and aspirations of its maker, or a robe that was emblazoned with the pictographic history of the wearer’s battles and was sometimes used in re-enactments. Also autobiographical, and indicative of high status within the tribe, would have been a tepee painted with symbolic designs to record the achievements and display the dreams or visions of its owner, who was often assisted in the painting by other tribal members.",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:269:      body: "<p>Simply put, the author’s purpose tells us <strong>WHY</strong> the author wrote the passage. None of the passages that appear on the LSAT are there randomly. Each passage and even the paragraphs within each passage are there for a reason. Think about real-life situations; we give speeches or write essays to explain, persuade, incite, attack opponents, defend a position, or justify and provide a rationale for our actions. All writers write with a purpose in mind, unless you are making a shopping list/laundry list or making entries in a diary. Even then, you can argue that we make lists to help us memorize and write journal entries to reflect and remember.</p>\\n    <p>If the main point/thesis is <strong>WHAT</strong> the author is trying to get across to the readers, the author’s purpose is the <strong>WHY</strong> behind why the author is penning this passage in the first place. These are essentially two sides of the same coin.</p>\\n    <p>As we read through the passage, hints and clues will be scattered throughout the lines that will give us a rough idea of why the author wrote this specific passage. Our first task, when approaching a passage, is to constantly ask ourselves why the author is writing what they did.</p>",\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:283:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Until about 1970, anyone who wanted to write a comprehensive history of medieval English law as it actually affected women would have found a dearth of published books or articles concerned with specific legal topics relating to women and derived from extensive research in actual court records.</p>" },\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:286:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>This is a serious deficiency, since court records are of vital importance in discovering how the law actually affected women, as opposed to how the law was intended to affect them or thought to affect them. These latter questions can be answered by consulting such sources as treatises, commentaries, and statutes; such texts were what most scholars of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries concentrated on whenever they did write about medieval law. But these sources are <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">of little help</mark> in determining, for example, how often women’s special statutory privileges were thwarted by intimidation or harassment, or how often women managed to evade special statutory limitations. And, quite apart from provisions designed to apply only, or especially, to women, they <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">cannot tell us</mark> how general law affected the female half of the population—how women defendants and plaintiffs were treated in the courts in practice when they tried to exercise the rights they shared with men. Only quantitative studies of large numbers of cases would allow even a guess at the answers to these questions, and this scholarly work has <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">been attempted by few</mark>.</p>" },\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:291:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>Most medieval English court records are written in Latin or Anglo-Norman French and have never been published. The sheer volume of material to be sifted is daunting: there are over 27,500 parchment pages in the common plea rolls of the thirteenth century alone, every page nearly three feet long, and written often front and back in highly stylized court hand.</p>" },\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:513:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>In studying the autobiographies of Native Americans, <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">most scholars have focused on as-told-to life histories that were solicited, translated, recorded, and edited by non-Native American collaborators</mark>—that emerged from “bicultural composite authorship.” Limiting their studies to such written documents, these scholars have <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">overlooked traditional, preliterate modes of communicating personal history</mark>. In addition, they have <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">failed to address the cultural constructs</mark> of the highly diverse Native American peoples, who prior to contact with nonindigenous cultures did not share with Europeans the same assumptions about self, life, and writing that underlie the concept of an autobiography—that indeed constitute the English word’s root meaning.</p>" },\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:515:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>The idea of self was, in a number of pre-contact Native American cultures, markedly <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">inclusive</mark>: identity was not merely individual, but also relational to a society, a specific landscape, and the cosmos. Within these cultures, the expression of life experiences tended to be oriented toward current events: with the participation of fellow tribal members, an individual person would articulate, reenact, or record important experiences as the person lived them, a mode of autobiography seemingly more fragmented than the European custom of writing down the recollections of a lifetime. Moreover, expression itself was not a matter of writing but of language, which can include speech and signs. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Oral autobiography</mark> comprised songs, chants, stories, and even the process whereby one repeatedly took on new names to reflect important events and deeds in one’s life. <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">Dance and drama could convey personal history</mark>; for example, the advent of a vision to one person might require the enactment of that vision in the form of a tribal pageant.</p>" },\r\nprojects/lsatu/src\\bootcamps\\rc-bootcamp\\content.ts:517:        { kind: "quote", html: "<p>One can view as autobiographical the elaborate <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">tattoos that symbolized a warrior’s valorous deeds</mark>, and such artifacts as a decorated <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">shield that communicated the accomplishments and aspirations of its maker</mark>, or a <mark class=\\"rc-mark\\">robe that was emblazoned with the pictographic history of the wearer’s battles</mark> and was sometimes used in re-enactments. ## assistant

I found the current conditional activity implementation. It is already built around a semantic routing model for the credited answer, but the student-facing path is at risk of treating “said the exact phrase” as correctness. The important design choice is therefore what the drill is actually measuring: logical structure, verbal fluency, or both.

## Round 1

❓ **Q1 — What counts as correct?**: Should the drill grade the student’s exact wording, or whether the student preserved the logical relationship?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Grade meaning, not wording. “If A, then B,” “A guarantees B,” and “B is required for A” can be treated as equivalent only when they encode the same direction and strength. Exact phrasing should never be the pass/fail gate.

---

❓ **Q2 — What should the student actually submit?**: Which response should be the primary graded artifact?

1. Write the conditional on paper, then choose the matching diagram or structured answer on screen.
2. Build the diagram directly on screen using selectable pieces.
3. Type or speak an explanation in free form.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Use option 1 as the default. It preserves the physical diagramming habit you want, while the on-screen multiple choice gives the system a reliable, deterministic way to grade. Direct manipulation can be a later variation. Free-form speech should be practice only, never the authoritative answer.

---

❓ **Q3 — What role should “if / then / because” play?**: Is the verbal sequence itself a skill you want to assess, or is it mainly a self-explanation routine?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Treat it as a coached rehearsal with separate feedback. The drill should ask the student to say or choose: “If ___, then ___, because ___.” Grade the selected logical components separately from the quality of the prose. A student who says “because the first condition guarantees the second” has demonstrated the reasoning even if the transcript does not match your model sentence.

---

❓ **Q4 — How much scaffolding should the drill provide?**: Should every item require the full process, or should the drill move through stages?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Use a fixed progression:

1. Identify the indicator and the trigger.
2. Choose the arrow direction.
3. Choose the correct diagram.
4. Choose the contrapositive.
5. Explain the relationship using “if / then / because.”
6. Remove scaffolding over time.

This separates translation errors from diagramming errors and prevents one speech-recognition mistake from obscuring the real skill.

---

❓ **Q5 — What should happen when the student is partly right?**: Should an answer be simply right/wrong, or should the drill record distinct partial outcomes?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Record a small error taxonomy: correct, wrong direction/reversal, wrong negation, incomplete consequence, and explanation mismatch. Give partial credit for correct components, but do not let partial credit silently count as mastery. The teacher-facing record should show which component failed.

---

❓ **Q6 — What does “paper” mean operationally?**: Is paper meant to be the student’s actual diagramming workspace, with the app checking only a selected answer, or do you want the student to upload a photo of the diagram for review?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Make paper the workspace and the structured on-screen response the gradeable record. Do not require photo upload in version one. It adds friction and still cannot reliably establish whether the diagram is correct without human review. A photo could be an optional “show my work” attachment later.

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '// The Switchyard routes a conditional\'s two condition-groups onto two tracks:\r\n// the sufficient clause onto IF, the necessary clause onto THEN. To animate\r\n// that, the world needs the credited answer broken into its two clauses and\r\n// each clause\'s polarity, which is what this module derives.\r\n//\r\n// The credited answers are authored, not free-form: every Conditional Rephrase\r\n// item\'s first credited answer is written as "If X, then Y." The contrapositive\r\n// is authored too, but several are written in shorthand ("Tank fine, pumps\r\n// cycled."), so the flip is DERIVED from the parsed clauses and the authored\r\n// text is shown alongside as the credited plate rather than parsed.\r\n\r\nexport interface Clause {\r\n  /** The clause text as authored, with any leading negation left intact. */\r\n  text: string;\r\n  /** True when the clause is stated negatively; drives the polarity lamp. */\r\n  negated: boolean;\r\n}\r\n\r\nexport interface Routing {\r\n  /** The sufficient condition. Couples onto the IF track. */\r\n  ifClause: Clause;\r\n  /** The necessary condition. Couples onto the THEN track. */\r\n  thenClause: Clause;\r\n}\r\n\r\nconst IF_THEN = /^\\s*if\\s+(.+?),\\s*then\\s+(.+?)\\s*$/i;\r\n\r\n// Negation as it is actually written in the bank. Ordered longest-first so\r\n// "cannot" is not matched by "not" with a different span.\r\nconst NEGATIONS = [\r\n  "cannot",\r\n  "can not",\r\n  "does not",\r\n  "did not",\r\n  "do not",\r\n  "will not",\r\n  "would not",\r\n  "is not",\r\n  "are not",\r\n  "was not",\r\n  "were not",\r\n  "may not",\r\n  "must not",\r\n  "no longer",\r\n  "not ",\r\n  "n\'t",\r\n  "never",\r\n  "neither",\r\n  "nothing",\r\n];\r\n\r\n/** Whether a clause reads as negative, which lights the capsule\'s polarity ring. */\r\nexport function isNegated(clause: string): boolean {\r\n  const s = ` ${clause.toLowerCase().trim()} `;\r\n  if (/\\bno\\s+\\w/.test(s) && !/\\bno\\s+longer\\b/.test(s)) return true;\r\n  return NEGATIONS.some((n) => s.includes(n));\r\n}\r\n\r\nfunction trimTerminal(s: string): string {\r\n  return s.replace(/\\s*[.;]\\s*$/, "").trim();\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Parse an authored "If X, then Y." credited answer into its two clauses.\r\n * Returns null when the answer is not in that form, which the world treats as\r\n * "no routing available" and falls back to plain text rather than guessing.\r\n */\r\nexport function parseRouting(creditedAnswer: string): Routing | null {\r\n  const match = trimTerminal(creditedAnswer).match(IF_THEN);\r\n  if (!match) return null;\r\n\r\n  const [, sufficient, necessary] = match;\r\n  const ifText = trimTerminal(sufficient);\r\n  const thenText = trimTerminal(necessary);\r\n  if (!ifText || !thenText) return null;\r\n\r\n  // A sentence carrying TWO conditionals is not two clauses, and the regex above\r\n  // will happily hand back the whole tail as one. Item 62 parsed to a THEN of\r\n  // "the flood layer dates to spring; and if the match is trustworthy, then the\r\n  // stratum was sealed" -- an entire second rule inside one capsule, drawn on\r\n  // screen as though it were a single condition. Returning null is the honest\r\n  // answer: the world already falls back to the plain prompt and the authored\r\n  // answers, which is correct, rather than a diagram that is wrong.\r\n  if (/\\bif\\b|\\bthen\\b/i.test(ifText) || /\\bif\\b|\\bthen\\b/i.test(thenText)) return null;\r\n\r\n  return {\r\n    ifClause: { text: ifText, negated: isNegated(ifText) },\r\n    thenClause: { text: thenText, negated: isNegated(thenText) },\r\n  };\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * The contrapositive as the machine performs it: the capsules swap tracks and\r\n * their polarity inverts, in one movement. This is derived rather than parsed\r\n * from the authored contrapositive, because the flip has to be a transformation\r\n * the student watches, not a second sentence that appears.\r\n */\r\nexport function flip(routing: Routing): Routing {\r\n  return {\r\n    ifClause: { text: routing.thenClause.text, negated: !routing.thenClause.negated },\r\n    thenClause: { text: routing.ifClause.text, negated: !routing.ifClause.negated },\r\n  };\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * The indicator word the item turns on ("only if", "unless", "the only").\r\n * Authored as the first quoted span of the item\'s explanation, which is where\r\n * the bank puts it. Returned so the departures board can light it in place,\r\n * and only after the yard has routed.\r\n */\r\nexport function indicatorOf(explanation: string): string | undefined {\r\n  const m = explanation.match(/"([^"]{2,24})"/);\r\n  return m ? m[1] : undefined;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *\r\n * Signal faults\r\n *\r\n * The locked partial set is three named errors, and the spec gives each one its\r\n * own choreography in the yard. Which one a student made is DERIVED here by\r\n * comparing their parsed routing against the credited one, rather than asked of\r\n * the Coach: the Coach returns a verdict and prose, and reading an error type\r\n * out of prose would be guessing. A structural comparison either finds the\r\n * error or honestly returns none.\r\n * ------------------------------------------------------------------ */\r\n\r\nexport type FaultKind = "reversal" | "dropped-negation" | "trimmed-condition";\r\nexport type FaultSide = "if" | "then";\r\n\r\nexport interface Fault {\r\n  kind: FaultKind;\r\n  /** Which track carries the affected capsule. Reversal affects both. */\r\n  side: FaultSide | "both";\r\n}\r\n\r\n// Words that carry polarity rather than content. Stripped before comparing, so\r\n// "is not refueled" still aligns with "is refueled" and the difference surfaces\r\n// as a dropped negation rather than as two unrelated clauses.\r\nconst POLARITY_TOKENS = new Set([\r\n  "not",\r\n  "no",\r\n  "never",\r\n  "cannot",\r\n  "cant",\r\n  "dont",\r\n  "doesnt",\r\n  "didnt",\r\n  "wont",\r\n  "wouldnt",\r\n  "isnt",\r\n  "arent",\r\n  "wasnt",\r\n  "werent",\r\n  "neither",\r\n  "nothing",\r\n]);\r\n\r\n// Function words carry no discriminating content for alignment.\r\nconst STOP_TOKENS = new Set([\n  "a", "an", "the", "is", "are", "was", "were", "be", "been", "to", "of",\n  "by", "in", "on", "at", "for", "that", "it", "they", "them", "their",\n]);\n\nconst TOKEN_ALIASES: Record<string, string> = {\n  overnight: "night",\n};\n\nfunction stem(token: string): string {\n  const aliased = TOKEN_ALIASES[token] ?? token;\n  if (aliased.length > 5 && aliased.endsWith("ing")) {\n    const root = aliased.slice(0, -3);\n    return root.length > 2 && root.at(-1) === root.at(-2) ? root.slice(0, -1) : root;\n  }\n  if (aliased.length > 4 && aliased.endsWith("ies")) return `${aliased.slice(0, -3)}y`;\n  if (aliased.length > 4 && aliased.endsWith("ed")) return aliased.slice(0, -2);\n  if (aliased.length > 3 && aliased.endsWith("s") && !aliased.endsWith("ss")) return aliased.slice(0, -1);\n  return aliased;\n}\n\r\nfunction contentTokens(clause: string): string[] {\r\n  return clause\r\n    .toLowerCase()\r\n    .replace(/n\'t\\b/g, " not")\r\n    .replace(/[^a-z0-9\\s]/g, " ")\r\n    .split(/\\s+/)\r\n    .filter((t) => t && !POLARITY_TOKENS.has(t) && !STOP_TOKENS.has(t))\n    .map(stem);\n}\r\n\r\n/** Share of the longer clause\'s content words the two clauses have in common. */\r\nfunction alignment(a: string, b: string): number {\r\n  const A = new Set(contentTokens(a));\r\n  const B = new Set(contentTokens(b));\r\n  if (!A.size || !B.size) return 0;\r\n  let shared = 0;\r\n  for (const t of A) if (B.has(t)) shared += 1;\r\n  return shared / Math.max(A.size, B.size);\r\n}\r\n\r\n/** True when `short` says a strict subset of what `full` says, and materially less. */\r\nfunction isTrimmed(short: string, full: string): boolean {\r\n  const S = contentTokens(short);\r\n  const F = new Set(contentTokens(full));\r\n  if (S.length < 2 || !S.every((t) => F.has(t))) return false;\r\n  return F.size - new Set(S).size >= 2;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Name the student\'s error, or return null when their routing is credited-clean\r\n * or too far from it to classify. Null is a real answer: the yard then makes no\n * claim about which named mistake was made.\n */\r\nexport function diagnose(credited: Routing, student: Routing | null): Fault | null {\r\n  if (!student) return null;\r\n\r\n  const straight =\r\n    alignment(student.ifClause.text, credited.ifClause.text) +\r\n    alignment(student.thenClause.text, credited.thenClause.text);\r\n  const crossed =\r\n    alignment(student.ifClause.text, credited.thenClause.text) +\r\n    alignment(student.thenClause.text, credited.ifClause.text);\r\n\r\n  // Both clauses sit on the wrong track: the reversal, the error this drill\r\n  // exists to kill. Requires a real crossed match, not merely a weak straight one.\r\n  if (crossed > straight && crossed >= 1) return { kind: "reversal", side: "both" };\r\n  if (straight < 1) return null;\r\n\r\n  // Routed correctly. Anything left is a fault inside a capsule.\r\n  if (student.ifClause.negated !== credited.ifClause.negated) {\r\n    return { kind: "dropped-negation", side: "if" };\r\n  }\r\n  if (student.thenClause.negated !== credited.thenClause.negated) {\r\n    return { kind: "dropped-negation", side: "then" };\r\n  }\r\n  if (isTrimmed(student.ifClause.text, credited.ifClause.text)) {\r\n    return { kind: "trimmed-condition", side: "if" };\r\n  }\r\n  if (isTrimmed(student.thenClause.text, credited.thenClause.text)) {\r\n    return { kind: "trimmed-condition", side: "then" };\r\n  }\r\n  return null;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/** Whether the student\'s routing differs enough from credited to be worth showing. */\r\nexport function differs(credited: Routing, student: Routing | null): boolean {\r\n  if (!student) return false;\r\n  const same = (a: Clause, b: Clause) =>\r\n    a.negated === b.negated && contentTokens(a.text).join(" ") === contentTokens(b.text).join(" ");\r\n  return !(same(student.ifClause, credited.ifClause) && same(student.thenClause, credited.thenClause));\r\n}\r\n\r\n/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *\r\n * Exact structural matching\n *\r\n * A student who writes an answer the author already wrote down needs no model\r\n * to confirm it. This recognises exactly that case and nothing else.\r\n *\r\n * It compares against EVERY credited answer, not just the first. In this bank\r\n * the second credited answer is the contrapositive, and on items 46, 47 and 48\r\n * it is a genuinely different logical direction. Checking only slot 0 would\r\n * grade the answer the task explicitly asks for as wrong.\r\n *\r\n * This narrow helper remains useful for exact authored structures. The\n * authoritative local judge below handles ordinary paraphrases and named\n * conditional-logic errors without calling the Coach.\n * ------------------------------------------------------------------ */\r\n\r\n/** Bumped whenever the rules above change, so shadow telemetry is attributable. */\r\nexport const PARSER_VERSION = "routing-2-local-judge";\n\r\n/**\r\n * True when the student\'s routing is structurally identical to one of the\n * item\'s credited answers. False is not a claim that the answer is wrong.\n */\r\nexport function matchesAnyCredited(creditedAnswers: string[] | undefined, response: string): boolean {\n  const student = parseRouting(response);\r\n  if (!student) return false;\r\n  return (creditedAnswers ?? []).some((credited) => {\r\n    const routing = parseRouting(credited);\r\n    return routing ? !differs(routing, student) : false;\r\n  });\r\n}\n\nexport interface LocalConditionalVerdict {\n  verdict: "correct" | "partial" | "incorrect";\n  feedback: string;\n}\n\nconst MIN_CLAUSE_ALIGNMENT = 0.67;\n\nfunction normalizedSentence(value: string): string {\n  return value.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, " ").trim();\n}\n\nfunction aligned(a: Clause, b: Clause): boolean {\n  return alignment(a.text, b.text) >= MIN_CLAUSE_ALIGNMENT;\n}\n\nfunction samePolarity(a: Clause, b: Clause): boolean {\n  return a.negated === b.negated;\n}\n\n/**\n * Judge a Conditional Rephrase answer entirely on-device.\n *\n * The prompt requires an If X, then Y form, which gives this parser a closed\n * grammar. Content words are compared by overlap after light normalization,\n * while direction and negation are checked separately. This accepts harmless\n * additions and ordinary inflection without turning a shared noun into proof\n * that a different claim is correct.\n */\nexport function judgeConditionalResponse(\n  creditedAnswers: string[] | undefined,\n  response: string,\n): LocalConditionalVerdict {\n  const answers = creditedAnswers ?? [];\n  const normalized = normalizedSentence(response);\n  if (!normalized) {\n    return { verdict: "incorrect", feedback: "Write the statement as If [sufficient], then [necessary]." };\n  }\n\n  // The final bank item contains two conditionals. Exact authored wording is\n  // still deterministically knowable even though the one-routing visual parser\n  // correctly refuses to squeeze two rules onto one pair of tracks.\n  if (answers.some((answer) => normalizedSentence(answer) === normalized)) {\n    return { verdict: "correct", feedback: "" };\n  }\n\n  const student = parseRouting(response);\n  if (!student) {\n    return {\n      verdict: "incorrect",\n      feedback: "Use one complete structure: If [sufficient], then [necessary].",\n    };\n  }\n\n  const candidates = answers.map(parseRouting).filter((routing): routing is Routing => Boolean(routing));\n\n  for (const credited of candidates) {\n    const sameContent = aligned(student.ifClause, credited.ifClause) && aligned(student.thenClause, credited.thenClause);\n    if (\n      sameContent &&\n      samePolarity(student.ifClause, credited.ifClause) &&\n      samePolarity(student.thenClause, credited.thenClause) &&\n      !isTrimmed(student.ifClause.text, credited.ifClause.text) &&\n      !isTrimmed(student.thenClause.text, credited.thenClause.text)\n    ) {\n      return { verdict: "correct", feedback: "" };\n    }\n  }\n\n  for (const credited of candidates) {\n    const straight = aligned(student.ifClause, credited.ifClause) && aligned(student.thenClause, credited.thenClause);\n    if (straight && (!samePolarity(student.ifClause, credited.ifClause) || !samePolarity(student.thenClause, credited.thenClause))) {\n      return {\n        verdict: "partial",\n        feedback: "Your clauses are on the right tracks, but a negation changed. Check every not, no, never, and cannot.",\n      };\n    }\n  }\n\n  for (const credited of candidates) {\n    const reversed = aligned(student.ifClause, credited.thenClause) && aligned(student.thenClause, credited.ifClause);\n    if (reversed) {\n      return {\n        verdict: "partial",\n        feedback: "You reversed the arrow. The necessary condition does not guarantee the sufficient condition.",\n      };\n    }\n  }\n\n  for (const credited of candidates) {\n    if (isTrimmed(student.ifClause.text, credited.ifClause.text) || isTrimmed(student.thenClause.text, credited.thenClause.text)) {\n      return {\n        verdict: "partial",\n        feedback: "One side of the conditional lost part of the original condition. Carry the whole condition across.",\n      };\n    }\n  }\n\n  return {\n    verdict: "incorrect",\n    feedback: "The direction or content does not match the statement. Find the trigger, then identify what it requires.",\n  };\n}\n\r\n/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *\r\n * The flip, and where it is safe to perform\r\n *\r\n * `flip()` swaps the clauses and inverts the polarity flag, but it cannot\r\n * rewrite the clause TEXT -- so a flipped rail used to read "the beacon goes\r\n * dark" with the lamp lit, directly above a plate reading "If the beacon did\r\n * not go dark". They contradicted each other on screen, in the drill about\r\n * negation. That is why the flip was never performed.\r\n *\r\n * Making it true means showing polarity as readable text rather than as an\r\n * unlabelled lamp, which needs the clause split into a positive core and a\r\n * polarity. That split is only sound for some of the bank:\r\n *\r\n *   - "the string quartet is not"      an ellipsis; the core is a noun phrase\r\n *   - "lack impulsive onsets"          lexical negation, invisible to the list\r\n *   - "no fixture is shielded"         the bare no + noun path\r\n *   - "both X and Y" / "X rather than Y"   NOT [...] does not resolve the scope\r\n *\r\n * Rather than guess at those, an item earns the animated flip only by passing a\r\n * gate that is checkable in a test. Everything else shows the AUTHORED\r\n * contrapositive plate with a static junction and no derived rails, which is\r\n * not a downgrade: the authored sentence is correct by construction.\r\n * ------------------------------------------------------------------ */\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Negations that behave as auxiliaries: they sit between a subject and its\r\n * predicate, and swapping them for their positive form leaves a clause that\r\n * still reads. Longest-first so "cannot" is never matched as "can not".\r\n */\r\nconst POLARITY_PAIRS: [negative: string, positive: string][] = [\r\n  ["cannot", "can"],\r\n  ["can not", "can"],\r\n  ["does not", "does"],\r\n  ["do not", "do"],\r\n  ["did not", "did"],\r\n  ["is not", "is"],\r\n  ["are not", "are"],\r\n  ["was not", "was"],\r\n  ["were not", "were"],\r\n  ["will not", "will"],\r\n  ["would not", "would"],\r\n  ["has not", "has"],\r\n  ["have not", "have"],\r\n  ["had not", "had"],\r\n  ["may not", "may"],\r\n  ["must not", "must"],\r\n];\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Negation the list above cannot lift: it is carried by the verb itself, by a\r\n * determiner, or by a word with no positive twin. A clause containing any of\r\n * these is negative in a way this module cannot state positively, so it is\r\n * excluded rather than guessed at.\r\n */\r\nconst UNLIFTABLE_NEGATION =\r\n  /\\b(never|neither|nothing|none|nobody|nor|without|lack|lacks|lacked|lacking|absent|fail|fails|failed|unable|refuses?|refused|omits?|omitted|missing|no longer|barred|prevents?|prevented)\\b/i;\r\n\r\n/** Structures where a single polarity token cannot say what it applies to. */\r\nconst NEEDS_SCOPE = /\\b(and|or|but|rather than|instead of|both|either|unless|except)\\b|[;:]/i;\r\n\r\nexport interface Polarity {\r\n  /** The clause stated positively, ready to carry a polarity token beside it. */\r\n  core: string;\r\n  /** Whether the clause as authored is negative. */\r\n  negated: boolean;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Split a clause into a positive core and a polarity, or return null when that\r\n * cannot be done truthfully. Null is the load-bearing return: it is what keeps\r\n * an unsound flip off the screen.\r\n */\r\nexport function readPolarity(clause: string): Polarity | null {\r\n  const text = clause.trim();\r\n  if (!text) return null;\r\n  if (NEEDS_SCOPE.test(text)) return null;\r\n  if (UNLIFTABLE_NEGATION.test(text)) return null;\r\n  // The bare "no + noun" path: negation carried by a determiner, which cannot be\r\n  // lifted out without rewriting the noun phrase.\r\n  if (/\\bno\\s+\\w/i.test(text)) return null;\r\n\r\n  const lower = text.toLowerCase();\r\n  const hits = POLARITY_PAIRS.filter(([negative]) =>\r\n    new RegExp(`\\\\b${negative.replace(/ /g, "\\\\s+")}\\\\b`, "i").test(lower),\r\n  );\r\n  if (hits.length === 0) {\r\n    // No negation signal of any kind. Guard against the contracted forms the\r\n    // pair list does not carry rather than calling the clause positive.\r\n    if (/n\'t\\b/i.test(text)) return null;\r\n    return { core: text, negated: false };\r\n  }\r\n  if (hits.length > 1) return null; // two negations: not this module\'s problem\r\n\r\n  const [negative, positive] = hits[0];\r\n  const span = new RegExp(`\\\\b${negative.replace(/ /g, "\\\\s+")}\\\\b`, "i");\r\n  // An ellipsis ends ON the negation ("the string quartet is not"), so lifting\r\n  // it leaves a noun phrase rather than a clause.\r\n  if (span.test(text) && /\\b(is|are|was|were|does|do|did|has|have|had|will|would|may|must|can)?\\s*not\\s*$/i.test(text)) {\r\n    return null;\r\n  }\r\n  const core = text.replace(span, positive).replace(/\\s+/g, " ").trim();\r\n  if (!core || core === text) return null;\r\n  return { core, negated: true };\r\n}\r\n\r\n/** A routing whose every clause can be stated positively with a polarity beside it. */\r\nexport interface DerivedRouting {\r\n  ifClause: Polarity;\r\n  thenClause: Polarity;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * The contrapositive as readable structure: the clauses swap tracks and each\r\n * polarity inverts, with the core text carried across untouched. Returns null\r\n * when either clause cannot be read, which is the signal to show the authored\r\n * plate instead of a derived diagram.\r\n */\r\nexport function derivedFlip(routing: Routing): DerivedRouting | null {\r\n  const ifSide = readPolarity(routing.ifClause.text);\r\n  const thenSide = readPolarity(routing.thenClause.text);\r\n  if (!ifSide || !thenSide) return null;\r\n  return {\r\n    ifClause: { core: thenSide.core, negated: !thenSide.negated },\r\n    thenClause: { core: ifSide.core, negated: !ifSide.negated },\r\n  };\r\n}\r\n\r\n// Authored contrapositives are written loosely: only 13 of 62 use "If X, then\r\n// Y", while 36 drop the "then". This reads that looser form, and is used ONLY\r\n// to check a derived flip against what the author wrote. It never feeds the\r\n// display, which still requires the strict form.\r\nconst IF_COMMA = /^\\s*if\\s+(.+?),\\s*(.+?)\\s*$/i;\r\n\r\nexport function parseAuthoredContrapositive(answer: string): Routing | null {\r\n  const strict = parseRouting(answer);\r\n  if (strict) return strict;\r\n\r\n  const match = trimTerminal(answer).match(IF_COMMA);\r\n  if (!match) return null;\r\n  const ifText = trimTerminal(match[1]);\r\n  const thenText = trimTerminal(match[2]);\r\n  if (!ifText || !thenText) return null;\r\n  if (/\\bif\\b|\\bthen\\b/i.test(ifText) || /\\bif\\b|\\bthen\\b/i.test(thenText)) return null;\r\n  return {\r\n    ifClause: { text: ifText, negated: isNegated(ifText) },\r\n    thenClause: { text: thenText, negated: isNegated(thenText) },\r\n  };\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Whether this item earns the animated flip.\r\n *\r\n * The last condition is the one that makes the rest trustworthy: the derived\r\n * polarity must AGREE with the polarity of the sentence the author wrote. That\r\n * is not belt and braces. Items 52 and 53 turn on "takes the color unevenly" --\r\n * lexical negation carried by a prefix, invisible to any list of negation words\r\n * worth maintaining -- and the derived flip would have rendered a double\r\n * negative the student had to untangle. Checking against the author catches\r\n * that, and catches every other case the word lists have not thought of yet.\r\n *\r\n * An item that fails any condition is not broken. It shows the authored plate,\r\n * which is correct by construction.\r\n */\r\nexport function flipIsDerivable(creditedAnswers: string[] | undefined): boolean {\r\n  const [first, second] = creditedAnswers ?? [];\r\n  if (!first || !second) return false;\r\n\r\n  const routing = parseRouting(first);\r\n  if (!routing) return false;\r\n  const derived = derivedFlip(routing);\r\n  if (!derived) return false;\r\n\r\n  const authored = parseAuthoredContrapositive(second);\r\n  if (!authored) return false;\r\n  const authoredIf = readPolarity(authored.ifClause.text);\r\n  const authoredThen = readPolarity(authored.thenClause.text);\r\n  if (!authoredIf || !authoredThen) return false;\r\n\r\n  return (\r\n    derived.ifClause.negated === authoredIf.negated &&\r\n    derived.thenClause.negated === authoredThen.negated\r\n  );\r\n}\r\n\r\n'}]

Until\r\n   * now text only reached storage as part of a submitted attempt, so closing\r\n   * the tab mid-sentence on question 24 lost the sentence.\r\n   */\r\n  draftKey?: string;\r\n  /** Fired once, when the item is resolved (correct, revealed, or coach failure). */\r\n  onResolved: (order: number, finalVerdict: Verdict | null) => void;\r\n  /** Append-only attempt record; best-effort, errors are toasted not thrown. */\r\n  onRecord: (args: {\r\n    itemOrder: number;\r\n    responseText: string;\r\n    verdict: Verdict | null;\r\n    attemptNo: number;\r\n    judgeSource?: JudgeSource;\r\n    shadowFault?: string | null;\r\n    latencyMs?: number;\r\n  }) => Promise<void>;\r\n  onNext: () => void;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Per-item state machine: submit -> coach verdict -> (retry up to cap) ->\r\n * reveal credited answer -> next. Owns its own state; the runner remounts it\r\n * per item via a key so state resets cleanly.\r\n */\r\nexport function ActivityItemView({\r\n  item,\r\n  format,\r\n  isLast,\r\n  coach,\r\n  answerLabel,\r\n  answerPlaceholder,\r\n  creditedSecondaryLabel,\r\n  promptInWorld = false,\r\n  fastPath,\r\n  localJudge,\r\n  shadowFault,\r\n  initialAttempts = 0,\r\n  initialValue = "",\r\n  draftKey,\r\n  onResolved,\r\n  onRecord,\r\n  onNext,\r\n}: ActivityItemViewProps) {\r\n  const { judge, judging } = useActivityCoach();\r\n  const [value, setValue] = useState(initialValue);\r\n  const answerRef = useRef<HTMLTextAreaElement | null>(null);\r\n\r\n  // Debounced rather than per-keystroke: a draft is not durable state and does\r\n  // not deserve a write on every character.\r\n  useEffect(() => {\r\n    if (!draftKey) return;\r\n    const t = setTimeout(() => saveDraft(draftKey, value), 400);\r\n    return () => clearTimeout(t);\r\n  }, [draftKey, value]);\r\n  const [attempts, setAttempts] = useState(initialAttempts);\r\n  const [result, setResult] = useState<{ verdict: FeedbackState; feedback: string; covered?: number[] } | null>(null);\r\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\r\n  const [resolved, setResolved] = useState(false);\r\n\r\n  const record = (args: Parameters<ActivityItemViewProps["onRecord"]>[0]) => {\r\n    // The attempt is the durable record now; the draft has done its job.\r\n    if (draftKey) clearDraft(draftKey);\r\n    void onRecord(args);\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  const resolve = (finalVerdict: Verdict | null, reveal: boolean) => {\r\n    setRevealed(reveal);\r\n    setResolved(true);\r\n    onResolved(item.order, finalVerdict);\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  const handleSubmit = async () => {\r\n    const attemptNo = attempts + 1;\r\n    const startedAt = performance.now();\r\n    const since = () => Math.round(performance.now() - startedAt);\r\n\r\n    // Some Activities describe a closed formal system that can be judged\r\n    // locally.
