Only an exact structural match against a credited answer takes\r\n    // this path, which is why it can be trusted: it confirms the answer key\r\n    // rather than reasoning about it. Everything else falls through and is\r\n    // judged exactly as before.\r\n    if (fastPath?.(item, value)) {\r\n      setAttempts(attemptNo);\r\n      setResult({ verdict: "correct", feedback: "" });\r\n      record({\r\n        itemOrder: item.order,\r\n        responseText: value,\r\n        verdict: "correct",\r\n        attemptNo,\r\n        judgeSource: "fast-path",\r\n        shadowFault: shadowFault?.(item, value) ?? null,\r\n        latencyMs: since(),\r\n      });\r\n      resolve("correct", false);\r\n      return;\r\n    }\r\n\r\n    const res = await judge({ item, studentResponse: value, attemptNo, coach });\r\n    setAttempts(attemptNo);\r\n\r\n    if (!res.ok || !res.verdict) {\r\n      // Coach failed. The student is never left stuck, so the credited answer is\r\n      // revealed and the item ends -- but it ends UNJUDGED. It used to end as\r\n      // "incorrect", which put a red verdict on an answer nobody had read and\r\n      // then scored it as a zero for the rest of time.\r\n      toast({\r\n        title: "Coach unavailable",\r\n        description: "Showing the credited answer so you can keep moving. This one is not counted.",\r\n        variant: "destructive",\r\n      });\r\n      record({\r\n        itemOrder: item.order,\r\n        responseText: value,\r\n        verdict: null,\r\n        attemptNo,\r\n        judgeSource: "fallback",\r\n        shadowFault: shadowFault?.(item, value) ?? null,\r\n        latencyMs: since(),\r\n      });\r\n      setResult({ verdict: "unchecked", feedback: "" });\r\n      resolve(null, true);\r\n      return;\r\n    }\r\n\r\n    const verdict = res.verdict.verdict;\r\n    setResult(res.verdict);\r\n    record({\r\n      itemOrder: item.order,\r\n      responseText: value,\r\n      verdict,\r\n      attemptNo,\r\n      judgeSource: "coach",\r\n      shadowFault: shadowFault?.(item, value) ?? null,\r\n      latencyMs: since(),\r\n    });\r\n\r\n    if (verdict === "correct") {\r\n      resolve("correct", false);\r\n    } else if (attemptNo >= MAX_ATTEMPTS) {\r\n      resolve(verdict, true);\r\n    }\r\n    // else: leave unresolved so the Try again / Show answer affordances show.\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  /**\r\n   * Try again used to empty the box. With a twenty-word answer and a one-word\r\n   * miss that is a punishment for being close: the student retypes nineteen\r\n   * words they had right. The text stays and arrives selected, so starting over\r\n   * is still one keystroke away.\r\n   */\r\n  const handleRetry = () => {\r\n    setResult(null);\r\n    requestAnimationFrame(() => {\r\n      const box = answerRef.current;\r\n      if (!box) return;\r\n      box.focus();\r\n      box.select();\r\n    });\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  /**\r\n   * Bailing out to the credited answer ends the item, so the log has to say so.\r\n   * It used to write nothing: the replay then saw one sub-cap attempt, called\r\n   * the item unresolved, and served it again on the next sitting as though the\r\n   * student had never reached it. The row is written at the attempt cap, which\r\n   * is what "I am done with this one" means to the replay, and carries the\r\n   * verdict they actually earned so the score is unchanged.\r\n   */\r\n  const handleShowAnswer = () => {\r\n    // "unchecked" is not a verdict and must not be written to the log as one.\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '    const shown = result?.verdict;\r\n    const verdict = shown && shown !== "unchecked" ? shown : null;\r\n    record({\r\n      itemOrder: item.order,\r\n      responseText: value,\r\n      verdict,\r\n      attemptNo: Math.max(attempts + 1, MAX_ATTEMPTS),\r\n    });\r\n    resolve(verdict, true);\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  return (\r\n    <div className="space-y-5">\r\n      <StatementPanel\r\n        item={item}\r\n        showQuestionType={attempts > 0}\r\n        showPrompt={!promptInWorld}\r\n      />\r\n      <ItemPrompt\r\n        format={format}\r\n        item={item}\r\n        value={value}\r\n        onChange={setValue}\r\n        onSubmit={handleSubmit}\r\n        answerRef={answerRef}\r\n        onRetry={handleRetry}\r\n        onShowAnswer={handleShowAnswer}\r\n        onNext={onNext}\r\n        judging={judging}\r\n        attempts={attempts}\r\n        result={result}\r\n        revealed={revealed}\r\n        resolved={resolved}\r\n        isLast={isLast}\r\n        answerLabel={answerLabel}\r\n        answerPlaceholder={answerPlaceholder}\r\n        creditedSecondaryLabel={creditedSecondaryLabel}\r\n      />\r\n    </div>\r\n  );\r\n}\r\n// columns (instructions, items) and the rows of the three Activity tables.\r\n// They intentionally do not import the generated Supabase Row types for the\r\n// jsonb internals, since Postgres types those as `Json`; the client casts the\r\n// validated shape to these on read.\r\n\r\nexport type Verdict = "correct" | "partial" | "incorrect";\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Who decided an attempt\'s verdict.\r\n *\r\n * `fast-path` is a world\'s deterministic local verdict, settled with no model\r\n * call. It covers exact authored matches and formal parsers such as the\r\n * Switchyard. `coach` is the edge function. `fallback` is a\r\n * Coach outage, where the credited answer is revealed and no verdict is earned.\r\n * Recorded so the share of attempts that never reach the model is a measured\r\n * number rather than a claim.\r\n */\r\nexport type JudgeSource = "fast-path" | "coach" | "fallback";\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * What the answer column is showing about the last submission.\r\n *\r\n * "unchecked" is not a verdict and must never be scored as one. It is the state\r\n * an item is left in when the Coach could not be reached: the student answered,\r\n * nobody judged it, and stamping a red "Not yet" over it was a claim the app\r\n * had no basis for.\r\n */\r\nexport type FeedbackState = Verdict | "unchecked";\r\n/**\r\n * `abandoned` is a Read, Recall, Colour section she started again from the\r\n * library without finishing. Its rows are kept and it is never graded; it is\r\n * not `completed`, because completed with rows is exactly what gets graded.\r\n */\r\nexport type ActivityStatus = "assigned" | "in_progress" | "completed" | "abandoned";\r\n/**\r\n * How a student answers an Activity\'s items.\r\n *\r\n * `spoken-recall` is Read, Recall, Colour: she speaks rather than types, and\r\n * the unit she answers is a chunk of an item rather than the item itself. It is\r\n * listed here because the column is a plain string and every other format is,\r\n * not because the runner branches on it; the world does that.\r\n */\r\nexport type ItemFormat = "free-text" | "mcq" | "variation" | "spoken-recall";\r\n\r\n/** One authored prompt within an Activity. */\r\nexport interface ActivityItem {\r\n  order: number;\r\n  /** The statement the student must respond to. */\r\n  prompt: string;\r\n  /** Author-supplied accepted answer(s); ground truth for the Coach. */\r\n  credited_answers: string[];\r\n  /** Why the credited answer is correct; revealed after the attempt cap. */\r\n  explanation: string;\r\n  /** Optional grouping label (e.g. "Universal Statement"). */\r\n  category?: string;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * One conceptual unit of a chunk, and the only thing completeness is measured\r\n * against.\r\n *\r\n * Written in plain analytic language, never in the stimulus\'s own wording. A\r\n * recall is REQUIRED to be in the student\'s own words, so a detail phrased as a\r\n * quotation could not be matched without failing the own-words criterion at the\r\n * same moment.\r\n */\r\nexport interface ChunkDetail {\r\n  /** Authored position in the list, 1-based. */\r\n  n: number;\r\n  text: string;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * One step of a spoken-recall item: what the student reads, and what has to\r\n * survive when she says it back with the text hidden.\r\n *\r\n * Chunking rule, which is settled and not a per-item judgement call: default to\r\n * one sentence, allow smaller down to a comma or a third of a sentence, and\r\n * never combine sentences, because the first sentence\'s details are what get\r\n * lost when you do. Round down, not up. The one exception is a chunk that is\r\n * not yet a complete thought, which may take slightly more.\r\n */\r\nexport interface RecallChunk {\r\n  n: number;\r\n  /** Verbatim from the item\'s prompt. The chunks must reassemble it exactly. */\r\n  text: string;\r\n  details: ChunkDetail[];\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * A Read, Recall, Colour item: one whole stimulus, cut into chunks.\r\n *\r\n * `prompt` is the entire stimulus and is never shown whole to the student; she\r\n * only ever sees one chunk at a time, and only until she taps past it. It is\r\n * stored whole because the chunks are checked against it and because the final\r\n * cumulative round is scored against everything in it.\r\n *\r\n * `credited_answers` holds one entry: a model retelling of the whole stimulus\r\n * in plain words with every detail intact. It is the reference for that final\r\n * cumulative round, and the clearest single statement of what "close enough"\r\n * means here. Per-chunk there is no credited answer, only `details`.\r\n */\r\nexport interface RecallItem extends ActivityItem {\r\n  chunks: RecallChunk[];\r\n  /**\r\n   * The chunk numbers after which the student says back everything so far with\r\n   * nothing on screen. The load grows every round, which is the point of the\r\n   * drill and the part software can enforce that paper cannot.\r\n   */\r\n  cumulative_after: number[];\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * How a spoken-recall Activity is run.\r\n *\r\n * `stimulus` is v1: one stimulus at a time, with cumulative rounds. `section`\r\n * is the captured drill, where the unit of work is a whole LR section run\r\n * unbroken against a clock, with no cumulative round and no coach in the loop.\r\n * Absent means `stimulus`, so every Activity authored before section mode keeps\r\n * behaving exactly as it did.\r\n'}]

rendered the same generic hero panel.\r\n * This is the wiring that was missing.\r\n *\r\n * What a world replaces is the LEFT column of the running screen, the cover\r\n * rail. The answer box, the Coach and the verdict loop on the right are\r\n * untouched: a world is the surface an Activity is worked on, not a different\r\n * way of working it. The wings (SortingYard, TriggerGallery, Assay, and their\r\n * kin) are separate mini-rounds and are not part of this path.\r\n *\r\n * `render` returns null whenever the item does not parse into the world\'s\r\n * domain. The caller then falls back to the generic hero panel, so an Activity\r\n * whose content drifts away from what its world can read degrades to the plain\r\n * surface instead of crashing or drawing an empty set.\r\n *\r\n * Progress ornaments are fed real per-item verdicts, replayed from the saved\r\n * attempt log, via `mileposts`. A world that draws none simply ignores the\r\n * argument; a Stage handed nothing renders nothing rather than faking a run,\r\n * which stays the honest default for the worlds not yet wired to it.\r\n */\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * One marker per item in the run. `outcome` is the verdict the student actually\r\n * earned, or null for items not yet reached: a world never renders progress it\r\n * cannot trace to a real answer. Replayed from the saved attempt log, so it\r\n * spans every sitting rather than only the current one.\r\n */\r\nexport interface RunMilepost {\r\n  order: number;\r\n  outcome: Verdict | null;\r\n}\r\n\r\nexport interface WorldRenderArgs {\r\n  item: ActivityItem;\r\n  /** What the student wrote for this item. Used sparingly, a dot and an\r\n   * eyebrow, never a fill: the surface stays monochrome editorial and the\r\n   * accent only says which world a card leads into.\r\n   */\r\n  accent: string;\r\n  /**\r\n   * Whether this world shows the item\'s text as written.\r\n   *\r\n   * Six of the eight do: the plate, the field note, the statement card, the\r\n   * bed and the woven cards all carry the prompt as written, so the answer\r\n   * column repeating it puts the same words on screen twice, side by side. At\r\n   * passage length that is two full paragraphs read twice.\r\n   *\r\n   * The Atelier and the Chamber do not: they take the argument apart, so the\r\n   * panes and the claim cards are a reading of the prompt rather than the\r\n   * prompt. The student still needs the original sentence to answer, and it\r\n   * stays in the answer column for those two.\r\n   */\r\n  carriesPrompt: boolean;\r\n  Entrance: ComponentType<{ onDone: () => void; title?: string }>;\r\n  render: (args: WorldRenderArgs) => ReactElement | null;\r\n  /**\r\n   * A local, authoritative check that the student has written an answer the\r\n   * author already wrote down. True means `correct`, decided without a model\r\n   * call; false is not a claim that the answer is wrong, only that it cannot be\r\n   * settled here and the Coach takes it. A world whose answers have no authored\r\n   * structure omits this and always asks the Coach.\r\n   */\r\n  fastPath?: (item: ActivityItem, response: string) => boolean;\r\n  /** An authoritative deterministic judge. Its presence disables AI judging. */\r\n  localJudge?: (item: ActivityItem, response: string) => { verdict: Verdict; feedback: string };\r\n  /**\r\n   * What the world\'s structural classifier WOULD have called this answer.\r\n   * Recorded next to the verdict the Coach actually gave, and never shown or\r\n   * scored: it exists so a future decision to trust the classifier can be made\r\n   * against a real disagreement rate instead of an argument.\r\n   */\r\n  shadowFault?: (item: ActivityItem, response: string) => string | null;\r\n  /** Names the ruleset `shadowFault` used, so a rule change stays attributable. */\r\n  parserVersion?: string;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * What a library card wears above the title: a name, a scope and an accent.\r\n *\r\n * Every Activity worked on a surface takes its world\'s. Read, Recall, Colour\r\n * has no world by design (ADR 0009) and, at Joshua\'s call, no themed name\r\n * either: it asks the student to stop looking at the screen, so inventing a\r\n * room for it works against the point. It still needs to be told apart in the\r\n * list, though, so it carries what it actually is instead of a place that does\r\n * not exist. Without this it is the one unlabelled row among labelled ones,\r\n * which reads as a bug rather than as a different kind of thing.\r\n */\r\nexport interface CardIdentity {\r\n  label: string;\r\n  scope: string;\r\nimport type { ComponentProps } from "react";\r\nimport type { ItemFormat } from "../api/types";\r\nimport { AnswerPanel } from "./AnswerPanel";\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Format-polymorphic right-column renderer. Negation Station uses the\r\n * `free-text` renderer (AnswerPanel). `mcq` and `variation` are stubbed for\r\n * later Activities so the player itself never needs to change to add a format.\r\n */\r\ninterface ItemPromptProps extends ComponentProps<typeof AnswerPanel> {\r\n  format: ItemFormat;\r\n}\r\n\r\nexport function ItemPrompt({ format, ...answerProps }: ItemPromptProps) {\r\n  switch (format) {\r\n    case "free-text":\r\n      return <AnswerPanel {...answerProps} />;\r\n    case "mcq":\r\n    case "variation":\r\n    default:\r\n      return (\r\n        <div className="flex h-full items-center justify-center rounded-[24px] border border-dashed border-border bg-card p-9 text-sm text-muted-foreground">\r\n          This item format is coming soon.\r\n        </div>\r\n      );\r\n  }\r\n}\r\n'}]

## assistant

Checkpoint succeeded. The key finding is that the browser cannot safely judge unrestricted paraphrase without a language model. That is not a missing clever regex. It is an information problem: “same meaning” depends on synonymy, scope, and grammar that a closed rule set cannot reliably infer.

A no-AI solution is still very workable, but it changes what the student submits. The app should grade a logical representation assembled from authored choices, not arbitrary prose.

## Round 2

❓ **Q1 — Are you willing to make the gradeable answer structured?**: The student would still do the reasoning, but the app would ask them to assemble the answer from authored pieces:

- choose which idea belongs on the IF side
- choose which idea belongs on the THEN side
- choose the direction
- preserve or select the correct negations
- handle `and` / `or` where relevant

The app then compares those selections to the item’s answer key exactly.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Yes. This is the clean boundary. The drill can teach “put it in your own words,” but only the structured logical representation should determine correctness.

---

❓ **Q2 — What should the structured interaction look like?**: Which feels most like the skill you want?

1. **Two-slot builder:** the student drags or taps authored clause cards into `IF` and `THEN` slots, then selects the contrapositive.
2. **Multiple-choice ladder:** each item asks a sequence of small questions: trigger, necessary side, arrow direction, negation, and contrapositive.
3. **Choose the complete diagram:** the student selects one of several complete diagrams, including reversal, negation, and compound-condition traps.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Use a hybrid of 1 and 2. The main interaction should be a two-slot builder because it resembles diagramming. Before submission, use small controls for polarity and compound structure. The ladder can appear as feedback or as a scaffold for early items. Complete-diagram multiple choice alone is too easy to solve by pattern recognition and does not make the student construct the relationship.

---

❓ **Q3 — What does “in your own words” mean here?**: If the app cannot verify free paraphrase, should that instruction be removed from the graded task?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Separate it into two layers:

- **Gradeable layer:** construct the exact logical form from selectable components.
- **Practice layer:** an optional text box or microphone prompt saying, “Now say it naturally: If ___, then ___, because ___.” The response is saved as practice or not saved at all, but it cannot make the item wrong.

This preserves the teaching habit without pretending the app can judge language it cannot understand.

---

❓ **Q4 — Should the student construct the contrapositive too?**: The current activity asks for both the direct conditional and its contrapositive, but the existing implementation mostly treats the response as one free-text answer and stores the second answer as authored reference text.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Make the direct conditional the primary construction, then make the contrapositive a second deterministic construction. The app can show the transformation visually and ask the student to select the correct swapped-and-negated version. This directly tests the `and`/`or` change that free text currently handles poorly.

---

❓ **Q5 — How much authoring are you willing to do?**: To make this reliable, each item needs structured metadata in addition to the current answer strings, such as:

```ts
{
  sufficient: { text: "...", polarity: "positive" },
  necessary: { text: "...", polarity: "positive" },
  compound: "and" | "or" | null,
  biconditional: false,
  acceptedForms: ["direct", "contrapositive"]
}
```

For a student-facing builder, we would also author the distractor cards and label the error each distractor represents.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Author this metadata for the conditional set. It is a one-time content cost, and it is safer than adding a growing collection of brittle linguistic exceptions. The existing `credited_answers` can remain as display/reference text, but they should stop serving as the machine’s semantic model.

---

❓ **Q6 — Do you want all existing items in the first version?**: Some current items contain compounds, unusual negation, shorthand contrapositives, or more than one conditional. Those are exactly the cases where free-text detection is least trustworthy.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Launch the structured version first with the clean, single-conditional items. Put compound, biconditional, and multi-conditional items into a second authored tier once their metadata and distractors are deliberate. Do not silently force every existing item through the same two-slot model.

## assistant

The screenshot also exposes the exact product promise we need to fix: it says “in your own words,” but the current screen gives the app no reliable way to judge those words. I recommend changing that promise rather than weakening the grader with more heuristics.

## Round 3

❓ **Q1 — Is this the right replacement for the answer box?**: For the example on screen:

> You can’t master the LSAT without mastering conditional reasoning.

The student would see authored concept cards such as:

- master the LSAT
- master conditional reasoning
- does not master the LSAT
- does not master conditional reasoning

They would place two cards into:

`IF ______, THEN ______`

The app’s answer key for this item would be:

`IF master the LSAT, THEN master conditional reasoning`

The student would then choose or build:

`IF does not master conditional reasoning, THEN does not master the LSAT`

➡️ **Recommendation:** Yes. This is deterministic, teachable, and still tests the real operation: identifying sufficient versus necessary, preserving negation, and forming the contrapositive.

---

❓ **Q2 — Should the student be allowed to type the natural-language answer?**: If we keep the text box as the main action, we cannot promise reliable grading without AI. The alternatives are:

1. Remove free text from the scored path, but offer an optional “say it” or “write it” rehearsal after the structured answer.
2. Keep free text and mark it “self-check only,” with no correctness verdict.
3. Keep free text as the scored answer and accept that some equivalent answers will be misclassified.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Choose option 1. The current free-text box should not remain the main answer mechanism if the activity promises fair grading.

---

❓ **Q3 — What does “in your own words” need to accomplish?**: Is the goal mainly to prevent students from memorizing symbols, or do you specifically want them to practice producing a spoken sentence like “If A, then B, because A guarantees B”?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Keep the spoken/natural-language sentence as a short second step, but label it “Explain it” rather than grade it. The app can display the student’s constructed logic beside the credited wording, so the student can self-correct without the system pretending to understand speech.

---

❓ **Q4 — How should the first attempt work?**: Should students receive the full set of cards immediately, or should the activity teach the skill through staged prompts?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Stage it within each item:

1. “Which idea is the trigger?”
2. “Which idea is required?”
3. “Build the direct conditional.”
4. “Build the contrapositive.”
5. “Explain it in your own words.”

After several clean items, collapse steps 1 and 2 and show only the builder. The two-try limit can apply to the completed construction, while intermediate selections give coaching rather than consuming an attempt.

---

❓ **Q5 — How should distractors be generated?**: Would you be comfortable authoring distractor choices for each item, rather than asking the system to invent them?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Yes. Author each item’s finite answer state and its meaningful traps: reversal, inverse/illegal negation, dropped “and,” wrong negation scope, and biconditional where applicable. The app should never generate semantic distractors by guessing.

Answer these five, especially whether you’re comfortable replacing “in your own words” as the scored task. Then I’ll map the exact data model and screen flow for this current activity.

Yes, this is much simpler, and I think it solves the immediate product problem cleanly.

The platform does not need to understand what she wrote. It only needs to show the statement, give her a private paper-first instruction, then ask her to identify which authored answer line matches what she wrote. The app grades the selected line, not the handwriting.

## assistant

Checkpoint succeeded. Your proposal has a strong boundary:

- paper captures the student’s own production;
- the platform supplies the answer key and distractors;
- the platform grades only a click;
- no handwriting recognition, speech recognition, or semantic paraphrase detection is needed.

## Round 4

❓ **Q1 — What should one click represent?**: Should the student select:

1. one answer line, either the direct conditional or the contrapositive;
2. one complete answer pair containing both the direct conditional and contrapositive;
3. two separate lines, one for the direct conditional and one for the contrapositive?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Use option 3. It preserves the current activity’s two-part goal while keeping each decision easy to understand. The student writes both on paper, then selects the direct form and the contrapositive separately.

---

❓ **Q2 — What should the choices look like?**: Should the options be:

1. full natural-language answer lines;
2. arrows/diagrams only;
3. full answer lines with a small diagram beside each.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Use option 1 for the first version. The activity is called Conditional Rephrase, and the student should compare the sentence they wrote to answer lines. Add the arrow diagram after the text, not instead of it.

---

❓ **Q3 — Should choices stay hidden until the student commits to paper?**: The app cannot verify that she actually wrote first, but it can require a deliberate checkpoint such as “I wrote my answer” before revealing the options.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Add that checkpoint. It is an honor-system control, but it makes the intended learning sequence explicit and reduces the temptation to read the choices first.

---

❓ **Q4 — What should two tries mean?**: If the student selects an answer line and misses, should the activity:

1. give one more attempt with the same choices;
2. show feedback about the type of error, then allow a second selection;
3. reveal the answer immediately after the first miss?

➡️ **Recommendation:** Keep two tries, but do not give away the answer after the first miss. Say what to recheck, such as “Check the arrow direction” or “Check whether the negation belongs on the required side.” After the second miss, reveal the credited direct form and contrapositive.

---

❓ **Q5 — How much authoring is acceptable?**: Each item would need a deterministic answer key plus a small set of authored distractors. For example:

- correct direction;
- reversal;
- illegal negation;
- missing or altered condition;
- correct contrapositive.

➡️ **Recommendation:** Author four or five options per step, with distractors chosen to represent the error the item is teaching. Do not generate them algorithmically until the clean set has been tested.

Please answer these five. The main decision is Q1: whether you want the student to verify one written form or both.
